Studies in the Lankavatara Sutra (D.T. Suzuki, 1930)
==========================================================
Suzuki's 1930 Studies in the Lankavatara Sutra — his landmark scholarly exposition of the sutra central to Zen's origins. Public domain. Source: Internet Archive.

PREFACE


While preparing a second series of Fsvaye in Zen Bud-
dhism, the author thought it desirable for the reader to know
something more about the Latkdvatdra than had been
cketehed out in the First Series ‘To do this he had to study


the sutra more thoroughly, and as he was doing so, his


interest in it grew stronger and wider. Then, he eame to
the veneclusion that his study of the Lankavatira might, be
published independently and even prior to the second series
of Zen Essays. The result is this book now before the
reader,

The Lenkévatdra is a Mahayana text difienlt in more
than one way to understand perfectly ax to its meaning and
also in ite proper historical setting. But its importance as
giving most of the fundamental tenets of Mahayana Bud-
dhism has urged the author to publish whatever results he
has gained so for in his study. They are no doubt short
of being quite satisfactory from a strictly scholarly point
of view, but the author's earnest wish is to open the why,
if he could so hope, for further study and more thorough-
going investigation of the text. Mahayana Buddhism is
just beginning to be known in the West As to the apprecia-
tion of it# full signifieance we have to wait patiently for
some Years yet to come.

The firet two parts of these Studies were already pub-
lished in The Eastern Buddhist, but in the present work
they have been revised fully and inaccuracies corrected As
fur us available. The third part is entirely mew. As the
Studies were not planned out as a whole from the beginning
but have grown progressively in the author's mind, some
repetitions have become inevitable, The second part dealing
with the Ledkévotdre containing the philosophy of Zen Bud-


vi LANKAVATARA SUTRA


dhism was written first. As it was being revised after its
publication in TAe Eastern Buddhist, Volume IV, Nos, +4,
for 1928, the thought suggested itself that the sutra must
be studied nlso textually since there are still three Chinese
aid one {or two) Tibetan translations, The result waw the
first part of the present work, which appeared as an
dependent article in Tha Eastern Buddhist, Volume v; No.
1, for 1929,

The Lenikdvatdra does not belong exclusively to the Zen
school of Buddhism, it is also the common property of the
Mahayans. When it is studied apart from Zen, some of the.
important conceptions developed in the sutra, which do not
necessarily belong to the philosophy of Zen, are to be ex-
pounded, however briefly. Hense the third part of the
present Studies, entitled “Some of the Luportant Theories
Expounded in the Laakdvatare."'

The author has prepared for the benefit principally of
his Japanese and Chinese readers a glossary of the Sanskrit
technical terms found in the book. This he hopes to bé
of use in their perusal of Sanskrit Buddhist literature and
it the same time illustrative of the methods of the Indian-
Chinese translators.

Tt is the pleasant duty on the part of the author to as-
knowledge all the help given him in various ways by the fol-
lowing friends: Mr. Dwight Goddard of Vermont, U.S_A,, who
typed the whole manuscript while he was staying in Satur
last winter; Professor Hikei Idzami who gave valuable
information. whenever the author met with grammatical
difficulties in reading the Sanskrit text: Mr. Bunkyé esis!
rabe who collected facts concerning the Tibetan translat
of the Lodkivatara: Professor Shizusato Sugihira and Mr.
Kensei Yokogawi who read the proofs for typographical
errors.

The author further wishes most sincerely to thank Mrs.
L. Adams Beek for her eritical reading of the proof-aheets
and for many valuible suggestions. He appreeiates the


REFACE


genuine interest «he has tuken in his attempt to intro
the West the Mahayana way of viewing human life and the
worid.


The work of proof-reading has also fallen to his wife who-
ig always ready to help the author in his literary unider-
takings, and for which he remains ever thanicful.

rinally, the anthor mentions with gratitude the sub-—
stantial help and encouragement ungrudgingly rendered by
his Friend, Mr. Yakichi Atuka, of Osaka, without whom this.
work would probably never have seen the light so soon and in
such favourable conditions. Whatever merit there is, ac-
eording to Mahayana teaching, in the produetion of such
work as this, may be transferred over to the further growth
of his spiritual welfare and also to that of all the author's
other friends and fellow-beings.

Dawerz Terraro SusvKi


Kyoto, September 1929

(2) That Sikehanatda and the Tibetan™ agree with the

What do these plain facts indicate? ‘The first logical
| inference is that Gupabhadra being the oldest translition
represents a more primitive Latkdvatdra than the others.
Possibly the later texts had these three extra chapters added
during the one hundred yeara that elapsed between Gnna-
bhadra and Bodhiraci.. That they were mechanically added


is shown by their having no organic connection with the

A comparison of these four texts will give us some
insight Into the mature of each version; the variations are
not necessarily due to the translators’ individualiam, they
mist have existed already in the original texts. Let me
give another parallelism, this time one in verse. The extructs
are from Chapter U, the opening githits of Mahiimati,. The
comparison will be only between the Tang md the Sanskrit,
as the Wei more or less agrees with the Sanskrit, while the
Sting agrees with the T’ang, though the Sung as well as
the Wei lack two versea corresponding to (4) and (5) of
ihe Sanskrit. ‘The most signifieant disagreement between
‘Yang and Sanskrit concerns ‘the awakening of o great
comptissionate heart.’’ Aecording to the Mahavanisis, 4
heurt is to be awakened m one that is above all forms of
attachment and yet that feels suffering in the world as its
own. In Sung and T'ang this idea ts omphatioally pre-
sented. whereas in Wei and Sanskrit it is missme. Froni
this: dan we not infer that there ware at least two quite
different texts of the Laakdvatdra from the early days of ith
existence ns far aa theae githas are concerned? I do not
lnow how the present Sanskrit text could be made to read
like Sung and T'ang, The philosophy of the Laikavatira
asserts the emptiness or the not-being-born of existence, and
it is quite right to say that the-world is like dream or
transcends birth-and-death, but we must remember that this
position is not one of absolute nihilism, bocause the sutra
teaches the reality of Prajia itself or the truth of the “*DMlind-
only’’ (citfamatra). So far the Sanskrit gathaa here re-
prodneed accord well with the principal ideas of the Ladkava-
fara, bot there is another element. in the Mahayana, which is
love or compassion, and when the world is surveyed from this
viewpoint, it is filled with sufferings, sorrows, and undesirable
events. These are also ins way dreamy happenings, but
compassion sees them in another light and strives to eradicate
them by all sorta of “skilful means,’’ For this reason, Sung
und ‘Tang are preferable here to Wei and Sanskrit.

Having finished what | wished to remark, though
sketchily, about those eliapters which are wanting in Giupa-
bhadra, and whieh, therefore, can logically be judeod as
later additions, I proceed to make some general statements
about the sutra-as to its form and contents and their inner
connections.

The text takes throughout a form of dialogue between
the Buoddbe and the Bodhisattvn Mahaimati, No other Bod-
hisuttvas or Arhats appear on the scene, though the dinlogno
is supposed to take place in an assembly of the Bhikshus and
BGodhisattvas asin other sutras. Gunabhadrn fixes the scene
of the sutra at the summit of Mt. Lanki in the Southern
‘Sea, but in it there is‘no mention whatever of Rivana, who,

8 LANKAVATARA SUTRA
in Bodhirnei and Sikshinands, plays an important role,
though in the first chapter only, as the initiator of the dis-
courses that follow. |

Mahamat? opens the dialogue by praising the virtues
of the Buddha, whose wisdom sees that the world is a shadow
but whose love embraces all suffering beings; Malimati then
proceeds to ask the World-hononred One about one hundred
and eight subjects: (cvhtottaram prasnaéatan), The Buddha
anawers; ‘“‘Let sons! of the Victorious One mek me, titi,
( Mahimati, vou too ask, and I will talk to you abgut my
inner realisation (pratyétmagatigocara).""

Now we ask, “What is the relation between’ the
Buddha’s inner realisation and Mahimati's 108 questions,
sbout which be wishes to be enlightened? Are all these
xubjecté concerned with the realisation itself t’" There must
be some connéction between the Buddha's replies and Maha-
mati’s questions, If not, they are certainly talking about
things of no concern to ench other,

Let us see, however, what questions issue from the lips
of Mahaimati now and what are the subjects he is interested
in. The queations are set forth in withia 12-09 inclusive,
in Chapter Il of the Sunskrit text. But what a eonglomera-
tion! Some-of them are, indeed, quite to the pomt aa they
refer, for instance, to the origin of intelleetion (farka) and
mental confusion (birdinii), and to their purification, eman-
eipation, Dhyana, Alaya-vijfiina, Manovijiiina, Cittamatra,
Non-ego, relative truth, phenomenality of existences, truth of
sucliness, the supreme wisdom (d@ryajidna), Buddha of
transformation, Buddha of Reeompense, absolute Buddha-
hood, enlightenment, ete. But at the same time there are
questions eonsterning medicine, certain mythical gardens,
mountains, woods, the capturing of elephants, horses, deur,
the gathering of clouds in the eky, rules of prosody, the six
seasons of the year, racial origina, ete. These do not seem
to be properly asked of the Buddha, who is not a college

« Jinapwtra, that je, Bodhinattvs,

professor, or rather a primary school teacher, but the master
of spiritual enlightenment. Why are the contents of the 108
questions of such a mixed character? |

What is more sstounding are the answers—that is,
answers that are supposed to enlighten the questioner—given
by the Buddha, The gathis 61-96 (molusive) are the words
of the Buddha, who is the wisest man in the world and who
is willing to diselose all the seerets of the Mahayana teaching
that have been tenght by all the Buddhas. He states in

“Birth, no-birth, Nirvana, emptiness-aspect, transforma-
tion,—[ull these are] without self-nature (asvabhdvatwa} :
the Buddhas born of Pairamita ;

‘“«Sravakas, sons of the Victorious One, philosophers,
formless deeds (aripyacirina) ; Mt, Samern, the great ocean,
mountains, isles. lands, earths;

“Stara the sun, the moon; philosophers, deities, and
dlso Asura: emancipation, Self-control, the Pavehic Facul-
ties, the Powors, Dhyiinns, Samadbis,

‘“Nirodha and the miracles, thy Dodhyangas, and even
the Paths: Dhyinas and Apramanas, Skandhas, and going
and ooming ;

‘“Sumapatti and Nirodhas-—they are mental disturb-
anees, only words, The mind, will, intelligence, uon-ego,
ihe five Dharmas—|ao are they too}.'™

So far, the answer, whatever be its exaet purport, is
more or less engent to the main ideas of the Lonkdvatdra;
ut what follows is strange not only from tho doctrinal
point of view but from literary construction. They are
often not answers but questions, some of which are mere
repetitions of the questions themselves, For mstance, the
Buddha is made to answer the 108 questions in this way:

“How are the elephant, horse, and deer caught! You

“What is meant by doing and being done? by various

iva bd

A glance is sufficient to see what kind of un answer this
is.. Questions and answers sre curiously mixed up, and
trifies and grave matters, too, The githis go on more or
less like this until the Buddha concludes thus:

"0 Son, thou askeat me suchlike ond many other ques-
tions. Each is in agreement with the [right] form, having
nothing to do with erroneous views, I will tell thee now
what the perfect dovtrine is. Listen to me! According to
the teaching of the Buddhas I will make. a declaration in
complete sentences of 108 clauses (padam). O Son, listen
thon to me (97-—98)..""

With what [right] form are the questions proposed by
Mahimati supposed to be in conformation? From what
erroneous views are they to be regarded as free? Whatever
we may say about them, one thing is sure that all these
questions and answers are incoherently strung together, and
we fail to find any logical interpretation to the whole body
of the githix making up the first part of the Lonkdvatara
Siltra.

Is some historical background needed to get a clue to
the solution? Another source of confusion is discovered
when we go on with Buddha's so-called 108 clauses, which

text of the Lankdvatdra is needed to understand it thoroughly

The Loikdvatdrw proper may be said to begin after the
theao “(Questions’’ onl “*Claukes"’, each 108 in number;

The study of the Leakdvatdre after Fa-ch‘ ung seems to

haa the power to rekindle the old and seek out new impres-

The Meaning of Yathdbhiltam and Maya


To understand the world and selfhood as they really are
—this ia seeing yathabhiiam, one of the great trumpet-calls:
ringing through beth Mahayana and Hinayana literature.
But to know exactly what ‘‘yathdbhatem"' means is the pro-
blem, for it does not allow of uny definite description, The
problem is to be settled only by appealing to experience, Ley
to direct perception when the truth has been grasped as such.
In this respect, Buddhist terminology is often graphic and full
of power; think of such terms as tathata (iri) or HA, 1 ide
suchness or thusness), taffvem (an jf or RU, thutnesa), or
satya (RR, being-so), which are used to designate the con
tent of the inner perception (pralydfmajidna). This se iz
yath@bhiitam constitutes the mystical element of all religion
and if one is affectively inclined, “‘Tat’’ (that) willl wave
to be taken in faith; but when the intellectual elaim pre
dominates, ‘*’Tat’’ will have to be perceived with ‘‘a nobl
eve of wisdom"’ (dryaprajilacakshus) and not by a divine
(divya) or human (mdse) eye joa

The world seen through a divine or human eye is a
world of maya, but one disclosed to the Prajfiai is the real
one, Therefore, logically speaking, miyd ia not a quality
objectively attached to the world, it is not inherent im it, it

What is Meant by Bomg Unborn?

createdness if it has any relative meaning stands in oppoui-

The Hinayanists do not know this; when they. realise
that Nirvana is something above particular conditions and
tranquil in nature, they feel that they have it in its eom-
pleteness in their Nirvana, so called. But in fact they dis-

cherish dualism, and so long as they do so, there ix no true

' Po, |


Nirvana for them, Nirvana, according to the Lonkdvatdra,
is not to be found in contradistinetion to birth-and-death
or Samsara; for this to distinguish one from the other is
the result of a wrong judgment (vikalpa), which imagines
® future when the world of the senses is altogether annihi-
Inted, which is their Nirvana, The Mahayanistic Nirvana
EOS, however, beyond the dualism of Nirvana and Samira.
It is to be found where there is the identity of Nirvana
and Samsara.

So long as dualism is adhered to, there ts no Nirvana,
nn self-realisation. Light and shadow, long and short, black
and white—they are mutually related; when they stand alone
each by itself, they have no meaning, So with Nirvana.
When it is sought after in relation to Samsira, we have a
sort of Nirvana, But this kind vanishes when separated
from the condition of mutuality in which it exists. True
Nirvana is that which is revlised in the oneness of Nirvana
and Samsira, absolute or simya in its nature, and above the
relativity of eternaliam and nihilism. Mahayana followers
atrive to renlise this kind of Nirvuna.'

they are without seclf-cubstance (diman). Ingamuich as the

The two horns of a cow are distinguished one from the

The World-Transcending Knowledge


The inner conseiousness of the Boddha, which con-
stitutes the essence of Buddhahood (svabuddhabuddhata), 18
the highest form of knowledge (jiidnc). OF knowledge the
Leakdvatara distinguishes three forms: (1) worldly know-
ledge (jidnam laukikom), (2) supra-worldly knowledge
(lokottaram), and (4) supreme supra-worldly knowledge
(lokottaratamam), The first is relative as cherished by
ordinary minds whose thinking is determined by idena af
being and non-being; the second is one possessed by Hina-
vanista who cannot go beyond the entegories of particularity
(svalakshana) and generality (sdmanyalakshana) ; while the
the third and highest is the knowledge attained by the
Ruddhas and Bodhisuttvas who view the world from an
absolute standpoint, for they know that the world is bevond
all eharacteristies, that is, has never been brought into ex-
istenee and will never be annihilated, that it is designable
neither as being nor a8 non-being. It is by means of this
highest knowledge that the Bodhisattva finally comes to
the realisation of the egoleasness (mairdfmya) of all things,
thus entering upon the path of Tathagatahood.* This


' Pp. mm, 72. ' P, 156 ff.


supreme supra-vorldly knowledge is none other thun the
supreme wisdom (dryajidna) of the Bodhidsattva, whieh
enfibles him to enter into the inmost mature (xvepratydtmea)
of all the Buddhas, and which constitutes the eeutral theme
of the Letkavaldre-siiee, Its three aspects are now dit
tinguished.’ The first is its not being mere appearance
(nirdbhdsa), that it is reality, as distinguished from the
way it is regarded generally by the [linayanists and philos-
ophers. The second is that it is awakened by the will
(pranidkdna) and power (adAishthdna) of all the Buddhas.
That is to say, supreme wisdom is aroused in the mind
of the Bodhisattva by virtue of the Buddhas" earnest
desire for universal enlichtenment and salvation; their
earnest desire or will sends ont strong waves of vibration
throughout the universe, and all sentient beings there feel
its effect according to their capacities, Thirdly, sustained
by this and going beyond the relative knowledge of the
Hinayanists, the Bochisattva’s mind is freed from all pred-
ieable forms and is ready for realising mm himeaelf a psyehie
state termed Miyopamisamidhikaya (gj XJ=GES). This
means the body attnined by uo Bodhisattva when he enters

considered as belonging to the Dharmaté-Buddha, who is

The Highest Knowledge and o First Cause

We know now that supreme wisdom realised in the

EXPEMIENCE

Having elucidated to @ certain extent though not so
exhanstively as a thorough survey of the Lunkdvatira may
require, as regards its general contents and especially the
nature of the inmost consciousness of the Tathagata known
as Pratyitmiryajiigocara, let us now proceed to see what
intellectual equipment is needed for a Bodhisattva before hie
ean attain to this inner realisation so emphatically acclaimed
inthe sutra. This intellectual equipment consisting of two
parts, logieal and psychological, is in a way the philosophical
eontent of the intuitive experience attained by the Bodhi-
sittva. Tt mav be regarded either as the intellectusl ath-
tude to be acquired by him before he enters upon the path
of Buddhist discipline, or as the philosophy of what he has
realised, which aya ratiomal being he is to elaborate later
on. In either case, the Ladkavatara offers us wn thorengli-
going idealism along with the message of self-realisation,
end this has been the point of discussion as referred to before
among the Buddhist exegetiste who wished to decide which
was the more important topic of the sutra, Whatever this
may be; we arenow prepared to see what is the philosophical

most essentinl teelaical terms in Buddhism. As it is used

means in given context. Etymologically, it comes from

always to be something of the idea ‘to endure’’ also going

The Twofold Non-Atman Theory,

We now come to a third distinctive feature of thie philos-
ophy of the Laakavatdra, which ts known as the twofold non-
Atmon theory, ic, Nairitmyadvaya.t The non-Atman or
non-ewo theory Is known among all Buddhist students ns
the most differentiating mark of Buddhism, but the denial of
an Atman or self-substance (svabkdva) in external objects is
the specific property of the Mahayana and may require some
explanation. To translate diman always by ‘Sean! or “*aell’
or ‘‘soul’’ may not be right; and especially when ite denial
is applied to an objective world, egolesaness has no meaning,
it is merely the source of misapprehension, An Atman
means something substantial in possession of a number of
qualities; and a free agent not bound by the principle of
relativity, When its existence, therefore, is denied in us, it
means that we have no ‘such free agent within ourselves,
enjoying a substuntial existence even above the concatena-
tion of enuse and effect. When we deny its reality in the
world external to us, it means that there ia no self-substance
(xvabhdva) in individual objects which come into existenee,
abide for a while, aud finally disappear according to certain
laws. In this case, nairdfmya is nihsvebhdva, and when it is
understood in this way, the idea falls in harmoniously with
the other views maintained by Mahayanists. Though not
yet formulated numerically by Buddhist scholars, there are
four distinguishing marks in Mahayana ontology which con-
stitute its very kernel. They are, (1) that all things are
empty (Sanya), (2) unborn (anufpanna), (3) not dual
(udvaita), and (4) without self-substance (niksvabhava)*
This sums up the metaphysical aspect of Mahayanism, and the
dual nor-Atman theory is merely a partial recapitulation of it.

The Doctrine of Mind-only

Having finished that phase of equipment which may be
called logical, for the upward career of the Bodhisattva, let
ns now pass on to the psychological phase, that is, the doe-
trine of eonaciousness, technically known as the theotpy of
the eight Vijnafius. As 1 wish to repeat, Buddhism being
a religion has no abstract interest in logic, or psychology, or
metaphysics per s¢, and especially in the case of the? la-
kavatdra the chief problem is to reach a stute of self-realisa-


.


m0 LANKAVATARA SUTRA


tion which ia the sine qua non of Buddhahood, and of Bodhi-
sattvahood as well. All efforts are to be directed’ towards
this goal, and it would be entirely against the spirit of the
sutra to disenss the psychology of the Ladkdvatdra, T am
doing this simply for the benefit of the modern reader who
wants to get a better perspective of the text than in its
original confusion,

Psychology is; however, the most difficult part of the
Lutkdvutero, and it is not an easy matter to get a Clear
insight into the meaning of it. What I have done here may
not be quite correct as far as the reading of the text is eon-
eemed, and I am open to conviction from the hand of a
more competent interpreter.

Most Buildhist acholars are often too ready to make a
too sharp distinetion between the Madhyamika anid the Yoga-
tira sehool, taking the one as exelnsively advocating the
theory of emptiness (Sinyatd) while the other is bent single-
mindediy on an idealistic interpretation of the universe.
They thos further assume that the idea of emptiness is not
at all traceable in the Yooaecdra and that idealism ig absent
in the Madhyamika. This is not exact as a matter of his-
torical fact. Though it is impossible just at present to fix
the date of the Laikivafdra definitely, we find im it the
tendencies that might haye developed into the Yogieaira a
well as into the Madhyamika; and these tendencies must be
regarded as prior to the development of these two. schools
each distinotly claiming its special province of interest. The
differentiation must have taken place after the sutra and not
before: for it is natural to infer that compoxite tendencies
appear first and then their decompesition into ROpRESHE ideas
and further development each along its specified ti

However this may be, the main point we must never
forget.in the study of the Laikdvaldra is that tt is not writ-
ten as a philosophical treatise to establish a definite eystem
of thought, but is to diseourse on a certain religious ¢x-
perience, What philosophy or speculation it offers is only


incidental #4 an introdtetion or as in intellectual interpreta-
tion necessitated by the rationsl nature of humanity, This


latter phase of religions experience may be more predominant:


in Buddhism thon in some other religions, but it is not for us
to overlook the essence of the matter for the sake of its more
or less unimportant accessories, however inevitable they may
he in the understanding of it.

As was. definitely stated, the principal theme of the
Lavkdvatira i¢ Pratyatmiryajiinagocara, the state of con-
seiousness in which the inmost truth is directly presented in
one’s mind. This being an immediate perception of the truth
it cannot be imparted to others by means of logic, but without
it the pereeption itself ceases to be operative, which is the
vane thing as not existing at all or being unreal The ex-
perience itself is without content, and must be given to it by
the intellect to make it workable in our social living. The
psychology of the Laikdvatira is also to be treated thus,
that is to say, its doctrine of ‘‘Mind-only”’ (eiffamalra) with
all ite accompaniments is meant to explain the mental expert-
ence of the Bodhisattva. We have always to bear in mind
this subordinate position of psychology or logic or meta-
pliysies in the teaching of the Ladhdvatara.

the rise (utpdda), ubiding (sthilé), and. disappearance?
(niradhe) of the Vijiina. Of this there are two forms,

The Bodhisative and Social Life

The eentral theme of the Lankdvatira has been  ¢x-
plained aa being the attainment of an insight into the inmost
consciousmess, and to attain this we have seen that the
sutra approaches the subject in two ways, logical and psy-
chological. But as Buddhism is a religion and ag every
religion has its practical and social side, without which it
will lose its reason of existence, the Lavkdvatira alio pre-
pares the Bodhisattva for hs mission as one of the members
of a cooperative life. In fact, this is what distinguishes |
the Mahayana from the Hinayana, for the latter’s object of |
spiritual discipline does not extend beyond his own interest,
however exalted it may be in itself,—the object being the
attainment of Arhatship, a solitary saintly life. This is all
well as far as it goes, bot as we are all living within a most
ecomplicutely organised eommunal body, not excepting even
o Buddha or a Bodhisattva, we have to think of this side
of life. The conception of a Bodhisattva was thus inevitable.
Tf he attained to a state of self-realisation which he finds .
so full of peace, bliss, and strength, his natural desire is to
impart it to his fellow-beings. Technically, when he has
finished benefiting himself (dtmahitam),' his next step is
to go out into the world and benefit others (parahitan)+
In reulity, he earinot do good for himself without letting
others share in it. The autra, therefore, now proceeds to
tell the reader what is the practical, ie, social life of the
Bodhisattva, It may be said that the object of gaining an
insight into the inner truth of things is really to qualify
oneself for social work.

, ‘coocding, a question may be raised as to the

tum be the object of salvation, or upon whom any kind of

shiould try to save the world when the Intter is no more than

The Pratyekabuddha order comprises those who are in-
tensely interested in anything that leads them to the roalisa-

tude and have no attachment to things worldly. When they
hear that the Buddha manifests himself in o variety of
forms, sometimes.in group, sometimes singly, exhibiting
miraculous powers, they think these are meant for their own
order, and immensely delighted in them they would follow
and aceepl them,

The Tathagata order may be again divided into three:
those who gain an insight into the truth that there is no
individual reality behind what one perceives, those who know
that there is an immediate perception of the truth in one’s
inmost eonsclousnese, and those who perceive that besides
this world there ure a great number of Buddha-londs wide

of the Alaya, from which starts this world of particulars,

The Boditsattva'’s Vows and Hii Effortless Works

Aveording to his transcendental insight into the truth

When the Bodhisattva enters upon the first stage called
Joy, Pramuditi, in the career of his spiritual diseipline, he
makes the following solemn vows, Pranidhaina, ten in number,
which, flowing out of his most earnest determined will, are
a allinelusive as the whole universe, extending to the ex-
tremity of space itself, reaching the end of time, exhausting
all the number of kalpas (ages), and functionmg uninter-
ruptediy as long as there is the appearance of a Buddha.
The first is to honour and serve all the Buddhas, one and
all, without @ single exception; the second is: to: work for the
preservation and perpetuation of the teaching of all the
Buddhas; the third is to he present at the appearance of
each Boddhea, wherever and whenever it may be; the fourth

af the Dhurma; 7. To ask the Buddha to:stay on living in this
world; 4. 'To be always learning from the life of the Buddha,

(8) ‘The Bodhisattva vows to learn from the life of a
Buddies who in this saha-world ever since his awakening of
the thought of enlightenment have never ceased from ex-
ercising himself ungrndgingly, not even sparing his own life,
For the sake of universal salvation. His reverential attitude
towards the Dharma had been such-as to make paper of his
skin. a brush of his bones, and ink of his blood wherewith he
copied the Buddhist sutras to the amount of Mount Sumeru.
He eared not even for his life, how much less for the throne,
for the palaces, gardens, villages, and other external things?
By practising every form of mortification he finally attained
supreme enlightenment under the Bodhi-tree. After this,
he manifested all kinds of psychical powers, all kinds of
transformations, all aspects of the Buddha-body, nnd placed
himself sometimed among Bodhisattvas, sometimes among
Srivakes, and Pratvekabuddhas, sometimes among Ksha-
triyas, among Bruhmans, householders, lay-diseiples, and
sometimes even among Devas, Nagas, human beings, and
non-human beings. Wherever he was found, he preached
with perfect eloquence, with a voice like thunder, im ordr to
bring all beings into maturity according to their aspirations.
Finally, he showed himself as entering into Nirvana. All
these phases of the life of » Buddha, the Bodhisattva is
determined to learn as-models for his own life.

(9) In this universe life manifests itself in innumerable
forms, each one differing from another in the way of its
birth, in form, in the duration of life, in name, in mental


of dwelling, ete, However different thus they are, the Bodhi-

a good physician; if they go astray he will siiow them the
right path; if they are sunk in poverty he will supply them
with a treasure; thus uniformly giving benefits to all beings
aveording to their needs, Why does he do this! Because
the Bodhisattwa is eonvineed that by serving all beings he is
serving all the Buddhas, that by revering all bemgs, by
making them glad, he is revering and ginddening all the
Buddhas, Why! Because a great compassionate heart 1
the substance of Tathngatnhood. It iv because of all beings
that this compassionate heart is uwakened, and because af
this compassionute heart the thought of enlightenment is
awakened, and because of-this awakening supreme enlighten-
ment is attaimed, It is like a great majestic tree growing
in the desert: that it spreads out 50 luxuriously ibs stems,
branches, leaves, flowers in all direction, i ilne to the supply
of water under the ground, So with the majestic tree of
enlightenment growing in the wilderness of hirth-and-death,
the roots are deeply planted among all beings, while it
blooms out and beard fruit as Buddhas and Bodhisattvas.
That these roots are well nourished is due te the pouring of
the water of great compassion, without whieh there will
indend be no flowers and frnits of enhghtenment ms attained
by Buddhas and Bodhisattvas- Whyt Becanse of the water
of great compassion all beings are benefitted and perfected
ns to attain the highest supreme enlightenment. Therefore,
enlightenment is dependent upon beings. If there were no
beings, there would be no Bodhisattvas nttaining supreme
enlightenment, Thus the Rodhisattva must have a good


understanding in this. As lw saad with 1 daopartiality the
minds of all beings, he is able to perfect lis great com-
passionate heart, and as he moves among all beings with this.
great compassionate heart in aceordanee with their modes
of tiving and feeling and thinking, he is able to complete
his offerings to the Tathagatas. In doing this the Bodhi-
sattva will know no fatigue even to the end of the world.

from evil doings, practise all deeds of goodness, so that every

be opened for the gods and men, Uf there be any beings
who are wiffering the results of their evil karma committed
in the past, the Bodhisattva will be ready to sacrifiee himself
and hear all the paina for the miserable creatures in order
to release them from karma and finally make them realise
supreme enlightenment, In this transference of merit onto
another, the Bodhisattva knows indeed no fatigue to the end
of the world, ete.

Then concludes the Bodhisattva Samantabhadra, ‘* These
are, © aons of good families, the Bodhisattva-Mahasattva's
ten great vows."

also to be examined even though eursorily.

The Citta and tls Evolution

Now the question naturally suggesting itself is: what is

Let us see first what the Loikdvatdra means by Citta.
Citta as noticed elsewhere is used in two ways, general and
specific: Where it ia used in a general way, it is the name
grven to the gum of all mental activities, including both the
mind proper and jis yarious functions, But where the
mind is divided into Citta, Manas, Manovijiana, and the
five Sense-vijiiiinas, (eye, ear, nose, tongue, and body), the
Cittn gains a specific signification. Jt is the principle of
unification by which all the activities art understood a¥ issu-
ing from one centre. The Manas is diseriminating agency
by which the homogeneous, undifferentiated Citta is divided
into two parts; the one as the seer and the other as the a
the one as the grasping ego.and the other asan object grasped.
The Monas isnot only an intellective but also a econative
principle. The Vijiiana, that is, the Manovijiiina is separn-
ted from the Manas, only retaining the latter's intellective
funelion, and may be translated as the intellect: in whieh
ease the Manas may be regarded as corresponding to the
will and the affection. The five Vijianas are thus the five
senses which discriminate a world of individual forms, each
within its own serse-field,

When the Citta iv this considered in its specific dense,
it may seem to be an abstract principle devoid of content.
But, according to the Ladkdvatara, this is not the ease: for
the Citta is ri¢h in content, and just because of this inner
richness, It ia able to evolve ont of itself a world of infinite
woultitudinonsness. It is, indeed, an inexhaustible reservoir

The Citta and the Alayavijidna and the Atman /
Along with the conception of Citta there was that of


the Vijiiaina system, and also the deepening of the ego-idea-


Early Buddhists denied the reality of an ego-substance,
whieh wasdin accordance with their psychology, but the idea
of ego was not necessarily the same aa the assertion of self-
will or egotism; even when self-will was destroyed, the idea
remained. What was destroyed was the lower self and not the
higher self, the smaller self and not the larger self; for the
annihilation of the lower and smaller self was only possible
through the assertion of the higher and larger one: Bud-
dhists never thought of putting an end to whatever might


go under the name of self. The iden of ego-substance


(dimon) wus inimical to the development of the higher centre
of the individual, nor was it in harmony with the experience
of their religious life: How is the question of the higher
life to be solved, then? Where is it to be placed in the
system of the Vijitanas? With this question an absolute
Citta came to be separated from the empirical ego, and this
absolute Citta to be identified with the Alaya, which was
now made the foundation of the whole Vijiana group. So
we have “citfam-dlayovijidnem’" and this then furnishes
the reason of the inner realisation a4 taking place in the
Tathizatn-garbha.* The philosophers take the Tathigata-
varbha, or the Alayavijiina* for the ego, that is, the lower,
narrower, empirical ego, which is, however, far from the
teaching of the Buddha. The real immaculate ego, #ud-
dhisatydtman, going beyond the grasp af relative knowledge,
cunnot #o easily he understood and 80 readily be nsserted as
is done by the ignorint, We thus read (ee. 757-771) ;

These passages fre to be carefully weighed, for if othor-
Wise they dangerously verge on the doctrine of an individual
ego-substance which is persistently denied by Buddhists:
Malayand a3 well aa Hinnyana. The main idea is that there
fe a principle of consciousness from which the whole Vijfina
system evolves and is set in operation, but which is not to
be regarded as something individual residing in the five
Skandhas, Ordinarily, this principle unknown, invisible,
and bevend the grasp of the sense-vijiiinas is tuken for an
ego, und unenlightened people try to locate it in the body
just asthey try to take hold of the pleasant notes that issue
from the lute, or of the effective, eurative agency that is
hidden ina medicinal herb, Apart from the lute, the sound
ia non-existent; so with the eurative quality, it doas not exist


outside the herb itself, The presence of the principle ia to
be realised inwardly by intuition and not by a process af
analysis. ‘This intuition, in other words, the ‘wisdom nf not-
outfowings’* (jildnom-endsravam), constitutes the original
nature of the mind whieh is described in the above quota-
tions as “‘originally pure’’ (prekriti-prabhidsparam). If
this is eonuidered something concrete and individual, some-
thing separable from and capable of being picked oul among
objects of particularisation, anying, “‘ Here it is!’’ this will
be rexorting to dualistic diserimimation which is condemned
2 much in the Lakikdeutdra, At all events it ia evident that
there was historically a close connection between the ego
idea and the evolution of Alayayijiina.

The philosophers too describe the world as having been

Boddha describes the world as being born depending upon
lnorance, dexire, karma, and diserimmation, Whether crea-
tion by cumsation, or dependence, the difference is in names
only. Thus both the Buddha and the philosophers proceed
to teach the birth of external objects by external causation,
and thas there is no distinction to be made between the
Buddha’s-and the philosophers’ theory, The philosophers
assert thot the nine substances containing such as atom
(anu), Pradhina, Isvara, and Prajaputi are neither born nor
annihilated, while according to the Buddha all existence is

Proofs for the “‘Mind-only"

So far no special efforts have been made to prove why
there ia Mind itself and no external realities, The Lenkdea-
fare is not a philosophical treatise and naturally does not
profess to prove anything specifically in connection with the
teaching or stutement it propounds. But as we peruse the
sutra, we cannot help coming seross certam attempts af
tdvancing proofs for its main thesis: svacitadrisyomitram,
We will try to gather wp these attempts already alluded to,
though sporadically in previous pages, and present them in
A more systematic way.

1. That things are not what they seem is proved from
the analogy of o dream anid magical creations* When
Ravana, king of Latka, saw images of the Buddha ull around
him, which later disappeared, he thought, ‘Could this be
a dream? or a magical phenomenon like the castle of the
Gandharvas?’*" He reflected again, ‘'This is no other thin
the projection of my own mental creations.’" As we do not
truly understand things os they are, we separate the seen
from the seer, thus producing a world of dualities. ‘‘ Where
there is no false discrimination, one really sees the Buddha,"
As long ss we are in the dream, we do not realise that we are
all dreaming, that we are slaves of false diserimination, For

where we have been, The analogy of dream is quite o strong
argument against the reality of an external world, but it is
not at all effective for them who are actually dreaming.

So it is with magical creations. The Indians have been
noted for their skill im the art of conjuration, and there
are no people among whom the use of mintrams and

As long #s they are under his spell, there is no way of making.
them realise that they are the victims of hypnotism.

2. All things are relative and have no svabhava (snb-

one from snother, Things are nothing but relations;
anulvse them into their component elements and there will

of the mind! Thus the Citta seeing itself reflected is due to

Some Concluding Remarks
Having enumerated these considerations in the Lakkdve-
jaro that lead to the thought of the ‘*Mind-only,*" let us male
this remark in the way of coneluding this part of the present
chapter. It is this: The LeAkdvatéra waa not written with
the view of establishing « philosophical thesis to be enlled
pure idealistic monism; its central motive was to make us
realise that enlightenment comes when we are detached fram
the tyranny of language and discrimination, when we enter
into the realm of wndyrava (non-leakage or non-outflow-
ing),’ when, going beyond all philosophical reasonings, we
stand on the inner ground of consciousness and shine out in
our ‘own inner brightness. This being the tase, as | have
already remarked elsewhere, it would be unreasonable of us
to expect anything systematically or logieally thoroughgoing
in the Ladkdwetire, Even when all the threads of logio are
found in confusion, the Laakdvatdra will still insist an the
davtrine of ‘“Mind-only,"" saying that it is a fact of im-
mediate perception and that enlightenment or spiriinal
freedom comes upon one after realising this fact withim one
self, It will then be the business of the philosophers to

1 cannot conclude this study withoul referring, though
casually, to the difference between the doctrine of Citta-
mitra and that of Vijiaptimatra (or Vijiinamitra), the
latter being the thesis of the Yogaeira school of Buddhism
which was founded principally by Asanga and Vasubandhu.
In 1926, Professor Sylvain Lévi published Sthiramati’s com-
mentaries on the Vijfiapti-matratésiddht by Vasubandhu,
which were discovered by him in Nepal; and we have at
present two Japanese translations of it: one by Dr, Junjire
Takakusu and the other by Dr. Unrai Wogihnra. Besides
these two must important texts of the Youiciira, Professor
Lévi sometime ago (1907) published another Yogicira text
known as Sitrilankdra with his French translation, This
iw ascribed to Aanuga. Thus furnished with the principal
text-books of the VijfianamAtra doctrine of Mahayana- Bud-
dhism, scholars are now able to nak; How is the Cittamitra
of the Laikdvatdra to be distinguished fram the Vijiaina-
mitra? Or are they the same, only differently designated?
The following is given more to elucidate the Lawhdvatdra
position than to give a definite answer to the question. It
ia 4 most significant question deserving o fuller treatment
than we may discuss here."

inets experienced by the Indian Buddhist minds, that is,
they are miost direct statements besed upon the intuitive
knowledge these minds have gained regarding the religions
life, Their statements may be logically untenable or impos-
sible, but they have not lost sight of the facta or experiences


that have flashed through their minds, All their paradoxes,


contradictions; incomprehensibilities, and even all their ap-
parently nonsensical utterances must be reduced first of all
to their intuitions, Buddhist students are, therefore, re-

reveal their inmost secrets wrapped up in these uncouth or

Ne-birth, Transcendental Truth, and Eternity
Unthinkable

We know that there are two positions usually taken up
by the Mahayana thinker who attacks problems of ontology.
Unless we know which of the two positions le being upheld
by him ot the moment, we shall find him quite frequently eon-
tradicting himself. The one is that of conventionalism, and
the other if we put it so is a highly metuphysieal one. If
we desienate the first os o relative position, we may call the
latter the abwolute one, From the relative or conventional
point of view the Mahayana admits that there is causation
and the world moves on from one state to another in con-
. formity with the law, but it refuses to give this continuaned
on absolute value, for it is real only as far ss our senses
. gare concerned, Conventionalism or naive realism doea not
after all give real satisfaction to the deeper demands of the
religious conseiousness. So the Buddhists ask us to go
beyond this world of appearance where supposedly all births
: and deaths are objectively real. A realm beyond the senses,
. however, must be opened from within ourselves. When we
pursue our objective course they would say we come to no
end, one chain leads to another, the concatenation has no
beginning and no conclusion. We tow make « retreat and
dig deeper and deeper down into the very foundation-rovk
of consciousness: When we do this, as the Lankdvatdra says,
there finally takes place a revulsion (pardwritfi) apparently


SS een


: within ourown minds, There opens up.a new vista where

wr never expected anything religiously or spiritually con-

eonvietion hitherto altogether unknown. This ia the new
position sipersensual and absolute, this is the supreme
wisdom (dryojfana), the renlisation of whieh within our-
selves is so emphatically reiterated in the sutra. And it is
from this point of view that the world is now describable as
unborn, with no selfsnbstance, and unconditionally empty
and not ut all limited by causation. Birthlessness (qnnt-
pada) or emptiness does not belong to the domain of our:
relative «@maciousness, The sutra in thie respect ts quite
explicit lest the unwary should slip at this point.. The devel-

sehoot of the éoneeption of twofold truth, Samvpitti und
Paramirtha, or worldly and transcendental, is the natural
consequence of the positions taken up by the Daakdvatare.

The reference to the hare's horns requires some explana-

their reasoning on non-existence and emptiness, Ths the

knowledge, while the no-birth is one of the utterances
directly issuing from the highest religions experience in. the
Buddha's life, and it naturally transeends a world of more
sense-date, That the hare hes no horns gains its meaning
when it is contrasted with a cow which jis adorned with a
pair of horns. Therefore, this kind of non-existenee or
hirthlessness is » relative one and no analogy exists betweetl

sense in which this analogy fails to illustrate the no-birth:

a thing is contrasted no more exists, the contrast itself loses


it'aense. ‘There ia still another aspect to this analogy which
makes it irrelevant to the proper compreliension of the Bud-
dhiat doctrine af no-birth. That is to say, when the hare
alone ia considered in its absolute aspect, with no reference

absolute truth transcends the relative categories of being and.
nou-being as in the cwse of the hare’s horns, Not only the
abeenee of horns on the hare is a relative ussertion, but the
idea itself is a result of discrimination; When the here aloné

lessness of all things as maintained by the Mahayanisia’

The Positive Conception in No-birth

“Why wre all things unborn?

“Peenuse they are unpredicable as made or making,
because they have no creator.

“Why have they no self-substance!?

‘*Becanse when they are reviewed with the interior
intelligence (snebuddhi),* the categories such as indivi-
duality and generality ure inapplicable to them:

“Why are all things nelther departing nor coming?

“Because though they are characterised with the marks:
of individuality and generality, these marks, coming and
departing, neither come nor depart,

“Why do all things never cease to exist! |

“Because their not having self-subatance makes it un-
possible to take hold of them.

“Why are all things impermanent?

‘‘Beeause as soon as they take forms [or appear with
individualised marks] they assume the nature of imperma-
nence.

“Why ate all things permanent?

‘* Reeause though they take forms [or appear with indivi
dunfised marks|, they take [really] no such forms, and in
reality there is nothing bern, nothing passing away.’

3. THE TRIPLE BODY OF THE BUDDHA


The Doctrine Outlined

The dogma of the Triple Body (irGyc) 28 now ad-
cepted by all Mahayana -sehools of Buddhism in Chima;and
Japan is a late development in the history of Mahayans.
The dognis, before it was fully formulated, was only
tdumbrated here and there in the earlier Mahayana sutras,
It was, probably, not until the Yogicirn philosophy began
to be crystallised into a system by Asanga and hin predeces-
sors that the conesption of the Triple Body came to form. o
part of their programmic.,

According to the Yogaeiira philogophy, the Triple Body
is Dharmakiya (zk 2}, fe-shin), Samblogakayn (Alf, pao-
shin), and Nirminakiiyn (44.57, Aua-shén}, Dharma here
may be understood in either way as ‘‘reality,’’ or as *‘law-
civing principle," or simply as “‘law."’ Ndya means
“hody,** or “aystem.”’ The combination, dharmakdya, is
then literally a body or person that exista as principle, and
it has now come to mean the highest reality from whieh all
things derive their being and: lawfulness, but which in itself
transeends all limiting conditions. But Dharmakiya is not
a mere philesophical word, as is indicated by the term
“Liva,"’ which suggests the idea of personality, especially
aa it relates to Buddhahood. It belongs to the Buddha, it
is what- inwardly and essentially constitutes Buddhahood, for
without it a Buddha loses altogether his being. We may
regard the Dharmakiiya as corresponding to the Christian
iden of Godhead, The Dharmakdya ia also known as Sva-
hhivakiiyn, meaning “‘self-nature-body" (fy#edy, feil-hsing-
shén), for it abides in itself, it remains os such retaining its
-selEnature. Tt is in this sense the absolute espect of the
Buddle, in whom perfect tranquillity prevails.

The second Body is the Samibhogakays, which is ordi-
narily translated as Body of Recompens:, or Enjoyment.


Literally, “enjoyment”? is a better ward for aghbhoga, for it
comes originally from tho root, ba, ‘te ent,"’ “to enjoy,”
to which the prefix sem, meaning ‘together’ is added, Thus
Surhibhogakiva ia often done into the Chinese JE) (seng-
wung-shén), S}H ar (xhow-wung-ahen), or peep (chih-shén).
When we have 42}, Recompense or Reward Body for it,
the Chinese seem to point to another Sanskrit original, or
else it ia not quite a literal rendering, but given aceording to
ita derivative sense. For this Body of Enjoyment is attained
aa the result of or as the reward for a series of spiritual dis-
cipline earried on through so many kalpas: The Body thus
realised is the Surmbhogakiya, Body of Recompense, which
is enjoyed by the well-deserving one, i., Bodlisattva-Mahs-
native.

The Buiddhe as the Body of Enjoyment iw generally
represented as a figure enveloped in ull the glory of Buddha-


hood: for im him incarnated there is everything good and.
beautifal and holy accruing from the perfection of the


spiritual life: The particular features of each auch Gud-
dha may vary according to his original vows; for instance,
his environment, kis nome, his form, his country, and
his activity may not be the same; Amitibha Buddha hae
his Pore Land in the West with all the acoommotdations as
he desired in the beginning of his career as Bodhitsstiva;
| en with Akshobhya av deseribed in the sutra bearing
J his name.
This second Body of the Trinity is:sometimes known in
Chinese as Ying-ahén (WU), instead of Pao-shin (327).
The original Sanskrit term for yiny might not have been
tthe same as the one for poo. Ying means ‘‘to respond” and it
is at present quite difficult to ascertain what its original was,
for we havé not yet recovered, if this were possible, any
Sanskrit sutra in which this term is used. From sambhoga
or savibhogyae pe (ying) cannot be deduced, while the

chapter on ‘The Trilkiya’" in the Sbiahechrackien’ is miss-

The third Body ia the Nirmiinikiya, usually translated
as hwa-shén (db), which means ‘‘Body of Transforma-
tion,’’ or simply “Assumed Body.” The Dharmakiiya is
too exalted a body for ordinary mortals to come into any
conscious contact with, As it transeends all forms of limita-
tion, it cannot become an object of sense or intellect. We
ordinary mortals can perceive and have communion with this
absolute body only through its transformed forms. And
we perceive them only according te our eapacitics, moral and
spiritual, They do not appear tous in the same form. We
thus read in the Saddharma-pundarika that the Bodhisattva
Avalokiteévara! transforms himself into «0 many different
forms according to the kind of beings whose salvation he has
in view at the moment. The Kshifigarbha-sitra also men-
tions that the Bodhisattva Kshitigarbha takes. upon himself
a variety of forms in order to respond to the requirements of
his worshippers, The conception of the Nirminakaya is
significant, seeing that this world of relativity stands con-
trasted with the absolute value of Suchness which enn be
reached only by means of the knowledge of Suchmess. (fal Aa-
fajad), The essence of Buddhahood is the Dharmakaya, but
as long as the Buddha remains such, there ts no hope for the
galvation of a world of particulars. The Buddha hes to
abunden his original abode, and must take upon himself suck
forms as are conceivable and acceptable to the inhabitants of
this earth, The Holy Spirit emanates, aa it were, from
Absolute Buddhahood and is seen by those who ary prepared
by their previous karma to see him,

fee] that there must be a spirit-or soul or shadow that lives

The Vipaka-Buddha

The nature of a Vipaka-Buddha is that of a Nishyanda-
Buddha when thiv is anderstood in the sense of a result
flowing from an antecedent eause, that is, aa one of the ive
effects (peftteaphala), and not im the sense of something
secondary which issues out of a more primary aubstanes ;
tind the idea is closely eonnected with the theory of Karma.
Thik ia not difficult to see, for the theory of Karma always
requires sornething resulting from a cause; that in, according
to it, no act ean perish without leaving its effect in one way
or another, The theory of Karma is another name for the
law of eansation. A deed, whether. moral or psychological,
therefore, once committed in the inner conseioumess, or
by the physieal body, will never fail to bring, not only
on the doer or thinker himself, but upon those intimately
related to him some result some day, whose value will some
how correspond with the antecedent, Buddhiam, thus, has
developed an elaborate science treating of the law of caues-
tion in the moral world, This was the ease especially with
the warlier followers of the Buddha before the Mahavana
began to develop to its full strength. The Jataka tales
are compiled wholly with the view of acquainting wa with
practicn! narratives iInstrating the thears of Karma,

One of the forms in which the theory of Karma has
impressed iti marks.in Mahayana Buddhism is the eoncep-
tion of the Vipaika, or the Nishyanda Buddha. Tho result
of the moral discipline known as the six or ten Perfections
(pdramita) whieh every aspirant for Buddhahood ix required


to observe, must be visible |
Bodliisattvn. And as long aa a world of individual forms
(riipaloka) is to be accepted oven ae an inevitable onteome
discrimination (vikelpa), the result of the Buddhist devo-
tion must be told, slke, under this limitation. Then the
Bodhisattva ascends to Akanishtha Heaven where his coarser
body is transformed into a shining one with all the marks of
an ideally perfect personality. He 1s then no more A Bodhi-
sattva endowed with the coarse physical corporeatity
like ourselves, but an immortal god or Buddha endowed with
a will-made bedy (mangmayakdya) to which the Laakdvatira
makes frequent allusions.

There is, however, another conception here entering into
the make-up of o shining Buddha, which comes from ovr
inmost religious yearnings, By this | mean the deep human
longing for a body of tranafiguration. We are not satistied
with our corporeal existence, we are all the time oppressnd
ly the feeling of imprisonment, our spirit soars away from
this world of physical limitations, we long forever for a
manomayakiya (will-body). This physical body does. not
fully express the meaning of the spirit, it deranges, it
tyrminises. In fact all the religions struggles and sspirations
we experience in this life are ventered on the eontrol of this
body. 'Theosophists, Swedenborgians, and the 'Taoists, and
the Indian plilosophers—they all have the idea of an im-
niaterial body whieh we cin assume when we ere favoured
by a divine gift, or when our moral diseipline reaches its
culmination, This is in one sense our longing for immor-
tality. We immortalise all the great historical characters,
for we cxnnot be reconciled to the idea that their physical
ending means the end of all that has entered into their
being,

The Buddhas’ entrance into Nirvana by no means in-
dicated the passing of all that belonged to him. His diz
ciples could never make themselves believe that their Master
wae no more with them. The dogma of the Dharmuakaya


4


was the inevitable outcome of their psychology ns well as
of their logie. But the Dharmakiiya wan still: too abstract
ani could not give full antisfaction to their deep-seated
religious longings. “These Jongings so innate to the human
heart were eombined in the ense of the Buddhist with the
dovtrine of Karma, and os the result the Vipaka or the
Nishyanda Buddha, or Pao-shan (PLS), came into existence
in his ides! glorifieation. Amit&bha Buddhs, the Lord of
Infinite Light, who is one of these Paoshin Buddhas Kite
now securely in his Land of Bliss and Purity, surrounded by
hii worshippers and inviting us poor earthly struggling
mortals to join him there.

Between the Vipika-Buddha and the Nicmina-Buddha
it will take some more research to ascertain the historic:

SOME OF THE IMPORTANT THEORIES 1


‘understood in two ways; one from the standpormt of the
Buddha himself, whose loving heart cannot help resorting
to every possible means of salvation, and the other from
the standpoint of sinful mortals. who desire every possible
help from o power higher than themselves. ‘This miutality
has filled the world with Nirmita-nairmipika Buddhas.


Wherever we turn we come across one of these trans-


formations, and if we are earnest and sincere and long-
ing from the depths of the heart, we can see even the real
Buddha himself in and through them. This is an iden
pregnant with results both goml and bad. When it is not
judiciously bandied, religion becomes a mass of superstition
and idolatory grows rampant.

The relation of the three Buddhas to one another may
he understood in the analogy of an individual person. That
there is something ultimate making up the reason of this
individual existence is to he granted, because the very con-
ception of individuality: is impossible without postulating
something behind it, This something is variously desionated
in philosophy and religion, and also variously interpreted
and speculated on, Even the denying of the existence
of this something is in truth asserting it, for both denial
and nasertion are based on the same principle. We cannot
escape this, and what makes it thua inevitable for us either
to assert or deny it ia the principle of individuality. Tlits


eorresponds to the conception of the Dharmati, or when


personified, to the Dhamatdi-Buddha.

Now this individual peraon stands in every possible
manner of relationship to his fellow-beings, human or other-
wise. As Chuang-tei asya, a woman may he regarded by her
companions as the most beautiful person and as such admired
and adored and loved. But when she comes near « pond
and reflects her shadow on water, all the aquatic inhabitants
will fee away from her as 4 most dangerous enemy, What
i one man’s poison is another's elixir of life. The same
‘ndividual is not the same at all times ‘to all others. Even


i table standing im this room does not took ‘alike to all
spectators, As it is observed from different angles and with
different: frames of mind the object before them is not only
plivsically not of ‘the aame size and colour, but _


tags ice
lovically not at all of the sume value ond significance. In
the ease of a living person this variability, temporal anid
constant, reaches its limits. The person himself has not
epparentiy changed tut he assumes or seems to assume
different forms to his neighbours, May not this aupect of
bis being bo called his Nirmina value? In spite of all these


external and relational mutations, the individual has mot at


all changed to his own consciousness. ‘That he is himself he
at all times knows and he enjoya his personality. hia eor-
responds to the Vipika-Buddhu, or Sambhovakiya.

Every conscious being may thus figuratively or rather
metaphysiecally be said to be the owner of the Triple Body.
In the ease of thy Buddha, the doctrine is filled with reli-
gious significance and it has played a most important role
the development of Mahayens Buddhism, One thing we
fave to notice bere is the replacing of the Buddha-trinity
by thet of the Body (kaye). That Buddhist philosophers
heve come to talk of the Trikayna instead of Buddhatraya.
It is not a matter of mere change of terminolowy, it involves
a deeper meaning. The reason is that Kaiya basa more
synthestsing value, while Buddha suevesta more of indivi-
duality. The tliree different kinds ef Buddhahood make

4, THE TATHAGATA

Inow something of him when we luve investigated what
this inner truth is, for it constitutes the very esence af

any specific proofs as this, indeed, composes the eontents af
the Lavkavatare itself, But the Protyitmadharma alone of
itself! docs not make wp the whole being of the Tathagata

and confused and who desires to lead all beings up to the

aod Pratyekabnddhas, as we are repeatedly reminded im the

ie not what we expect of the Tathagata. He must show some-
thing more, he must be more than himself, go beyand his
Inner revelutions. This means that he must be a personality.

Oneness, Their emancipation ie made possible only by

have not yet mode themselves free from the hindrance of

formution-desth (acinfypariddmacyuli). For these reasons

4. plane which surpasses the state of non-outflowings where
they have hitherto remained."

2. The Pive Deadly Sinst
‘The following five deeds are regarded by Buddhism as
the worst offences to be committed hy its followers; (1)
murder of the mother, (2) of the father, (3) of the Arhat :

a. On Meat-rating (p- 244 et seq.)

The motive for having this chapter appended to the
Latkivatirg, when it has no organic connection with, the
text proper, seems to lie in 9 pawage towards the end, Ae:
cording to it, the Buddha evidently dislikes the idea of being
thougitt of as the one who has permitted meéat-eating among
his followers, He says, there may be some unenlightened


followers of mine after my death, who, not knowing the


spirit of my teaching and training, may wrongly conelode
that | allowed them ts eat meat and that I myself ate it.
This would be disastrous. For how can those who pre abiding
in 6 merciful heart, disciplining themselves. jn asceticism,
uu trying to follow the path of Mahayana, tell, others to eat
animal food? Indeed, I have elsewhere given rule as to
the eating and not esting of mest; ten rules for avoiding and
three rules for accepting it. But in this Lohkdeotdra ag
well aa in the Hastikakshya, Mahdmegha, Nirvina, and
Adgulimdlika sutras, Meat-eating is aheolutely forbidden.
Not only in the past, but in the future and now, all my
followers are to shun animal [nod no matter how it has been


prepared. If there is any one who would accuse me of


eating meat myself and allowing others to eat it, he will


surely be born in an undesirable region. Holy people


refuse to ent evon the food of ordimary people, how much
more so with meat-enting! Their food is the food of truth

SOME OF THE IMPORTANT THE
(dharmahira), the Tathagata’s Dharmakiya is supported by
that!


According to this, thire must have been the accepting of
mest-food among the followers of Buddhism in the time
when the Laikdvetdra was compiled. Evidently, the Bud-
dhe did not object to their eating it if the animal was not
especially killed for them. This caused unfavourable eom-
ments among the other religions, for instance, the Lokiyatas,
and the Buddhists naturally did not like them, and this
mins! have started the new effort to prohibit meat-eating
altogether among the Mahiyana advocates.

The following are the reasons for not eating
food as recounted in this sutra:

(1) “All sentient beings ore constantly going through
4 cycle of transmigration and stand to one unother in every
possible form of relationship, Some of these are living at
Present even as the lower animals, While they so differ
from us now, thoy all are of the same kind as ourselves.
To take their lives and eat their flesh is like vatine our own.
Human feelings cannot stand this unless one is quite callous,
When this [get is realised even the Rakshasas may vense
from eating meat, The Bodhisattva who regards all beings
as if they were his only child eannot indulge in flesh-eating.

(2) The essence of Bodhisattraship is a great eompas-
mi0nate heart, for without this the Bodhisattva logers his
being. Therefore, he who regards others as if they were
himself (sarva-bhitdtmebhilta), and whose pitying thought
(Fripitna) is to benefit others as well as himself, ought
not to eat meat. He is willing for the sake of the truth to
sacrifice himself, his body, his life, his property: he has no
freed for anything; ond full of compassion towards all
sentient beings ani ‘ready to store up good merit, pure and
free from wrong discrimination, how can he have any lone-
ing for meat? How ean he be affected by the evil habits
of the carnivorous races?

(3) This cruel habit of eating meat causes an entire
transformation in the features of a Bodhisattvs, whose skin
omits an offensive and poisonons odour. The animals are
keen enough to sense the approach of such @ person, n person
who is like a Rakshasa himeell, and would be frightened
and run away from him. He who walks in ¢onspession
(mottri-vihdri), therefore, ought not to ent meat.

(4) The mission of a Bodhisattva i4 to create among
his fellow-beinge a kindly heart and friendly regard for
Buddhist teaching. If they see him eating meat and cau
ing terror among animals, their hearts will naturally turn
away from him and from the teaching he professes. They
will then lose faith m Buddhism,

(5) It a Bochisattva eats meat, ho cannot attam the
end le wishes; for he will be alimated by the Devas, the
heavenly beings who are his spiritual aympathisers and pro-
tectors, His mouth will smell bad; be may not sleep
soundly: when he awakes he is not refreshed; his dreams are
filled with inauspicious omens; when he is in a deserted place,
ill ‘alone in the woods, he will be haunted by evil spirits;
he will be nervous, excitable at least provocations; he- will
be sickly; have no proper taste, digestion, nor assimilation:
the course of his spiritual discipline will be constantly inter-
rupted. Therefore, he who is intent on benefitting himeelf
and others in their spiritual progress, ought not to think of
partaking of animal flesh.

(6) Animal food is filthy, not at-all clean as W nonrisli-
ing agency for the Bodhisattva. It readily deeays, putrifies,
and taints. Tt is filled with pollutions, and the odonr of it
when burned is enough to imjure anybody with refined
taste for things spiritual,

(7) The eater of mont shares in this pollution,
spiritually. Onee King Simhasaudisa who was fond of
eating meat began to eat human flesh, and this alienated the
affections of his people. He was thrown ont of hia own
kingdom. Sakrendra, o celestial being, once turned himself
